[[The speaker put a yellow box entitled "No Austrian Tax Schilling for EU nuclear industry", in which stylized euro banknotes are located, on the speaker's desk.]] If one has pursued the internal policy debate over the last few weeks, one could gain the impression that it is a single issue, namely: Anti-atomic policy, resistance to Temelín. What is often too short and what undermines the credibility of these issues is the anti-atomic policy of the Austrian Federal Government, especially in Brussels, especially within the European Union. You must now imagine that, at present, half a billion Austrian taxpayers are going to be spent in the future for new nuclear reactors: for research, for new nuclear installations! In the UK, there has been a nuclear reactor since 1983. He differs from other nuclear reactors: It does not produce electricity and eats billions of money. – This nuclear reactor will have follow-up projects: A project that costs 45 billion Schilling, another project that will cost 110 billion Schilling. And the European Union will spend 17 billion schillings in the years 2002 to 2006 for the entire range of this mistake in the exploration of new nuclear reactors! 17 billion schilling from the taxpayers of European citizens! What is the Austrian position now?, is one entitled to do so. What is the Austrian resistance to prevent such a thing? What was the Austrian line in the negotiations for this billion-dollar programme, around this political mistake? And I'm asking you: Is that not in a certain contradiction to your domestic Temelín resistance? Is it not entirely unbelievable for Austria to run a completely unacceptable line in the great changes within the European Union, as is to be said in energy policy? Such projects, like this one: another 17 billion Schilling and another 500 million Schilling Austrian tax money, prolong the lives of the nuclear industry in Europe. And that's why it's so critical and absolutely incomprehensible that you haven't tried in advance to prevent that. [[Abg. Oils: The government parties are the problem!]] But we have – and I would like to say this in all clarity here – gone further than any government before us. Three nuclear power plants are now planned to close down, seven become safe – and clearly so! – retrofitted. Although there are no Western European safety standards yet, we have managed through the back door to introduce safety standards to the candidate countries for the east of Europe. We have started with knowledge and also with the approval of the House of Lords a year ago the Melker dialogue with which we have achieved things that have always been required by NGOs and many activists, namely an environmental impact assessment, a list of recognizable security failures, supplemented by recommendations from the "Atomic Question Group" as well as by Austrian experts who have submitted a whole list of proposals. What is yet to be achieved is that all these points – seven plus 21 – will also apply in the case of privatisation, and in the case of accession as Annex or Protocol to the EU Treaty will be binding and thus become an acquitable European law. That is why the wording "closes the energy chapter" is bothering me, because what it is about, ladies and gentlemen, is that we are opening a meaningful process that gives us the opportunity for the first time in the history of Temelín to secure our security interests in a sustainable way. Ladies and gentlemen of the opposition! Close You're not that all-Austrian consensus! Please cooperate, it would be in our country's interest.

Let me keep a few facts in all soberness: First: Each country is sovereign in terms of its energy policy – nothing else has been said by Federal Minister Molterer – unless this country grows up by its membership in a state community, such as the EU. Let's be honest: If the popular vote on Zwentendorf in Austria had been concluded several times, we would not have been forbidden by any neighbouring country to lock up and put into operation the power plant. Secondly, Temelín is only one of several cars in Austria. As much as some essential security issues remain unresolved in Temelín, it must be noted that Temelín is not more uncertain than some other nuclear power stations in our environment, which no human is talking about today. In other words: It would be an illusion to believe that only Temelín did not need to go into operation so that we can all sleep calmly again. Thirdly, The non-conclusion of the energy chapter is quite different from an explicit veto against the accession of the Czech Republic to the European Union. The conclusion of all 31 chapters negotiated with each candidate country constitutes a prerequisite for the accession procedure. Only in the context of the accession procedure must all the current Member States of the European Union and the European Parliament agree to the admission. And it is only at that time that each EU country will have the right to agree or not to take up a new member country. I therefore think it is counterproductive to make a veto today. If, through our veto, the Czech Republic does not become a member of the EU, and Temelín locks up without any security arrangements within the framework of its then unchanged, unlimited sovereignty, please, will we? Rather, our goal should be that this nuclear power station, like everyone else, should go into service once, meet the highest requirements, requirements that can only be reached by negotiation. This is the only goal-leading way that the Austrian Federal Government has taken, namely – starting from the milking process – to do everything to ensure that Temelín meets the safety standards that apply to other nuclear power stations, or not to let them go into operation. Apart from creating a constitutional problem here – after all, there are several judgments that suggest the abolition of the indirect federal administration only with a constitutional change, such as the 1987 ruling on the cellar inspection, in which the indirect federal administration is referred to as an essential element of Austrian federalism; or the 1999 Telekom ruling, in which the parliamentary administration has been referred to as the core element of the Austrian constitution; and beyond that, shift responsibilities to incompletely trained Quasi judicial bodies, which cannot meet a demand for justice, close to citizens, after taking into account the interests of the citizens, because they are suffocated in employment. And in this work, ladies and gentlemen, you have built up a special delicacy, which you really have to let go on the tongue: This is the fact that the district administration authority can now determine whether the appeal authority may decide subsequently in the matter at all. This is really against any legal understanding of an appeal authority that legal certainty for humans is not guaranteed, and above all there will be no reduction of proceedings. For example, if the District Headquarters keep writing in: You may only become cashier at the UVS!, then a procedure will run 15 times in the circle. This applies to economic operators and also to ordinary citizens. So, how They do so, therefore, will not reach the citizen, will not be realized close to the citizen. I can already understand you: In this matter, of course, you have encountered the resistance of the national leaders, because they cannot be put into their personnel policy. At the UVS, people are ordered temporarily. For the first time since 1945, the world has faced a completely new situation, namely that not the West alone, but the West will be able to face the global challenges together with Russia. Question 1: "How do you currently assess the status of negotiation... of the ‘Melker’ process and how do you want to achieve that the results of the bilateral negotiation process become legally binding and valid for Austria?" I refer to this very extensive study of the current negotiating position presented here by Environment Minister Molterer. Negotiations are currently under way, and we also have many intensive talks. Especially in these days, the Minister for the Environment has accidentally met his Czech counterparts on the verge of an integration council. On the margins of the UN General Assembly and in Brussels, of course, the Minister for Foreign Affairs has been in contact. Our experts are practically several times a month in Prague or Brussels to bring the negotiations to a good end. You know the most important concerns that will also be presented to you today in a joint motion for a resolution. These include the seven key security issues that Austria wants to know and which we have not yet been guaranteed by the Czech side. This can be achieved. However, this also includes the 21 measures to be implemented under the first environmental impact procedure and this assessment. We know that these are Austria's main negotiating goals. I am concerned – I have also said this publicly very openly, because I believe that the Czech side also has to rely on what we expect from it – that even in the case of any privatisation step – today the Czech government is the owners of CEZ, but it should, as we hear, be carried out internationally by the end of the year or by the spring – these results must be properly fixed and also apply to a new, private-law operator. To that end, I know that is the most difficult thing to do, but it is our objective, and it is our right to anchor these security measures within the framework of the Accession Treaty so that we can also sue them. I believe that the Czech government is well advised to address this Austrian issue, because if you agree on something – at least that has always been my principle – then you have to stand by it. Then there can be no problem that this is ultimately presented by European bodies for final control. On question 2: It asked the European Commission: "Why are the different assessments between Austria and the European Commission on the safety of the Temelín nuclear power plant?" Here I would point out quite clearly that the European Commission has for the first time taken over the role of a mediator, especially in the "trilogue" on security issues, which has affected these 29 issues. The representatives of the European Commission have repeatedly noted that there are open security issues related to Temelín, which is very important for our position. Until then, it was just: The Austrians, the nervous, we already know that! We have been confirmed by the Atomic Question Group and the European Commission that there are open security issues. But it's interesting – and that's the crucial question: Can these security questions be solved by reasonable, also complex retrofits? – In this respect too, hearing in the European Parliament is very interesting, which otherwise has not been a special success. However, there has been clearly confirmed by both Austrian and other experts that this is possible, but costs a lot. This means a very substantial change in Czech positions. Of course, there are different evaluations by experts; we know that. I would also like to point out that, unfortunately, there are still no uniform safety standards in the European Union. We have made a great effort to do this, and I would have expected Greens and Social Democrats to do something more in the framework of their party colleagues – for example in the Federal Republic, for example in France, for example in Scandinavian countries – than to coin small internal policy money. They could also be in favour of such binding European security standards. ParlaMint-AT_2001-11-21-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00083_d7e17673	High house! Ladies and gentlemen! I would also like to say a few words about the Treaty of Nice. After all, it was not quite a year ago that it was relatively tough negotiations that plagued us there. I think the result can be seen. First of all, we have created the conditions for EU enlargement, and secondly, we have also deepened EU integration, especially in those areas which have just been given enormous importance, such as the area of internal security, judicial cooperation, world trade. It has now been strongly demonstrated in Doha that a common European position is very important. This also applies to foreign and defence policy, where further development is urgently needed. This also has the consequences of the 11th. ==References====External links== We have kept further Austrian interests: We strongly support our ideas that each country must be represented in every institution and have been able to maintain these interests for at least a long time, both as regards the voting weight in the EU Council and as regards the number of Austrian parliamentarians in the European Parliament and the representation in the European Court of Justice. I think these Austrian interests are well guarded. We have for the first time created the prerequisites for a case like mumbling towards Austria with a new procedure – that is to say, Article 7 – that a case like this can never be repeated again. Now there is a party hearing, now there is an objective procedure, and now a country that feels unfair can resist it. It is a legitimate procedure, and the European Parliament too can speak and decide. I believe that these results of the Treaty of Nice are significant and have turned Europe into a very positive direction. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] I do not want to go into too much detail now; we have already discussed the details several times in the committee, but we have also dealt with this in the authorisation law for Nice. However, if you allow, I would like to say a few words because this was discussed in the Presidential Conference too, on the follow-up effects of the 11th. September 2001 says that a number of Austrian initiatives have been launched since then and that the situation can and should be assessed differently in the light of today's events. First of all, I am delighted to mention that within a few weeks, Europe has taken 80 different individual measures to take seriously the fight against terrorism: the drying out of the financial networks, the fight against the smiters – not in the harmless sense, but in the sense of an agent – and the cooperation of the secret services, the police authorities and so on. However, military actions have also been launched that have been justified from my point of view – and that has proved the events that have occurred since then – at all skepticism at the beginning. In the end, they were also, in the end, also under a great deal of protection for the civilian population – which cannot always be accepted from the outset, but it was our common wish – that went ahead. I also believe that the operations were so targeted and so effective that a relatively rapid result is visible. The Taliban has already been pushed back to very few support points from a large part of Afghanistan, and it has been implemented very effectively in the non-military area. First of all, a political initiative has been set up. The focus was on the Middle East: There will be no lasting peace in this whole region, until deep into Europe or Asia, unless the peace process in the Middle East gets a chance again. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and on representatives of the Free Trade Unions.]] The European Union has invested a lot, but Austria has also set up initiatives through visits. I would like to thank all those who have been involved. This is to be expected: Austria is supposed to bring itself in and try to do something. I believe that these initiatives have also been greatly appreciated. The second is that on the American side, the feeling is growing that the time has now come to set an initiative again. Colin Powell gave a fundamental speech yesterday. What impressed me very much was that in his first speech to the UN, American President George Bush spoke for the first time, historically, of two states in this region: Palestine and Israel. This seems to me to be a very important initiative. This is something we Austrians have said for many years: There will be no peace unless the Palestinian population gets its own fully developed state, which, of course, has to guarantee internal security and stability, but at the same time needs a chance of survival. I think this is an important initiative. We can fully support them! [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and on representatives of the Free Trade Unions.]] Thirdly, we have been very massively involved in re-evaluating relations with Iran. We have a real chance, because Austria has never broken down relations with Iran. The Austrian Cultural Institute was always present, was always open. When the Goethe Institute was blocked, we recorded all Goethe Institute teachers. In the Austrian AI, the only place in Tehran was the possibility to get to know western culture, literature, music and so on. This has grown in confidence, but also in the dialogue with the Islamic religious leaders and politicians, which Alois Mock has begun and which I have continued and which now leads Benita Ferrero-Waldner. This "dialogue of civilizations" has taken place several times and has led to a new basis of trust, which is also of great importance to the Americans and to the British. Then: Of course, it is not obvious that, for example, the US President or the British Prime Minister is drawing attention to these initiatives. This also explains why the visits I made in Washington and London have met with relatively great interest from the US President and the British Prime Minister. I believe that we should continue along this path, and I would like to thank all those who are involved here, and also make it clear to everyone that the efforts that have been put into intellectual, political and sometimes financial use here have been well established. I think Austria has really been able to position and profile itself here, and we can be proud of it together. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] Two more things. There has been something that has already been discussed in the Treaty of Nice: there are tendencies that some large countries could try to form a sort of directorate and almost put the other facts before it. I think this is totally wrong. I immediately contacted my government colleagues in the middle and smaller countries after this notorious dinner in London. We then launched a joint initiative. I went to Brussels, spoke to the Commission and to the Presidency of the Council, and I then used Tony Blair in London to stop this happening, because it is actually weakening the European position. I would also like to thank all those who have fully supported this Austrian but also very European position in the country and abroad. I mean, it's important to show flag in such a question. It has not harmed us, on the contrary, we are even more at the centre of the European idea and action. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] Ladies and gentlemen! The fight against terror is not over. I would like to point this out completely without passion and soberness. On the contrary: One is now slowly on it, because, of course, the criminalistic puzzles are now being merged, that there is a much wider network of El Kaida and other terrorist organizations than one has ever thought. It was arrested about a week ago in Spain an entire terrorist unit of eight people, a Spaniard and some Arabs. A mullah has had a very special meaning here: He went to London again and again to keep contacts with the political leaders around Osama Bin Laden. Telephone protocols have been made and are also published, from which the reference to the Osama-Bin-Laden-Terror network has been made quite clearly. Home searches have been carried out in Spain, which have clearly demonstrated that there have been concrete plans for terrorist attacks, bombings or poison attacks against other European cities. A headquarters of Osama Bin Laden has been discovered in Kabul, where instructions for the construction of smaller and simpler nuclear bombs could be found as well as instructions for the production of highly dangerous toxic substances. I am not saying this because of being afraid of this, ladies and gentlemen, or to launch a new wave in the fight for a surveillance state. This is why I am saying this in order to be more precise and professional here and to take these things more seriously than in the past; for example, we are thinking of warnings that have come from, for example, moderate leaders of the Arab world who have not been taken seriously by a very liberal foreign and asylum practice in European countries. Many have now realized that this is no longer the case, and are now starting to rethink radically. I do not believe that you should generalize, but the more precise and consistent you pursue these terrorist networks, the faster you can really eliminate this danger and, above all, more limited, even more time-limited these measures can be. But these are things that actually make us shudder after all, and I am very proud that we have been pursuing a very common line in Austria in the security, justice, foreign policy and in the military and other news sectors so that we have been able to protect Austria from such threats so far. I hope that this will remain so, and I also very much hope that the House will support the officials, but also the ministers responsible. The fight against terror is not in vain, as you can see, it is successful, but it is laborious and must also be led with hardness and determination.

I should therefore first of all like to make a formal motion for a resolution tabled by Members, Mr Ein, Mr Schweitzer, Mr Fassleven and Mr De Vries on the enlargement of the European Union, and I would ask that this request be distributed because it cannot be explained in every respect and cannot be presented at all. First of all, it is about seeing us – and to this extent a link to the process that has been initiated after Nice to a constitutional reform of Europe – to actually create a Europe in the interests of the citizens. That's what it's all about. And if this Europe is to be created in the interests of citizens, we believe that, on the one hand, it will need a much stronger democratisation of the EU and, on the other, a policy that takes seriously the needs and everyday care of people in this great Europe. This is one of the first steps to take seriously the concerns of enlargement and to give a real answer. We have therefore agreed to discuss and propose concrete measures for a strong economic location in Austria together. The aim is to ensure framework conditions that give Austrian companies, especially small and medium-sized enterprises, the real opportunity to continue to exist and to be successful under changed conditions of competition – not least in the interests of the employees employed there. Secondly, we have dealt with the issue of the labour market in a very detailed manner, and you know that we are in favour of a gradual and controlled opening of the Austrian labour market, but precisely for the careful opening of this labour market. We therefore think that it would be useful if there were to be a prelude to the full liberalisation of bilateral employment agreements. We think that it is necessary to gradually get used to the Austrian labour market, so to speak, by the end of this seven-year period – hopefully even earlier – full free movement. We have also dealt with the issue of freedom to provide services, because of course this is very closely related to the free movement of workers, and we have clearly agreed that, for example, the German experience with the agreements on work contracts there should be taken into account. We should not simply do one by one what the Germans have done, because the regulation there has proved to be a flop. We should find a better regulation based on the negative experiences of Germany. I think, in order to address one last point, that, of course, it is also necessary that, in the transitional period – in the seven-year or hopefully shorter transitional period – appropriate controls be carried out to ensure that what is to happen at this time, namely that no additional workers on the Austrian labour market are actually carried out. Ladies and gentlemen! Of course, we have also dealt with the issue of transport policy in detail. It is necessary to put in place a number of measures here. If, together, we are concerned that, with the enlargement of the European Union, there is a renewed boost in the multiplying of, in particular, truck freight transport, then it is urgently necessary to implement, for example, truck-road pricing before enlargement. It is not about taking away money from those who come from abroad and drive through Austria, how weiland the Kuenringer, but it is about actually getting to know shift effects and avoid effects. It is a matter of ensuring that unnecessary, uneconomic transports are not necessary and that the rest are carried out as environmentally friendly as possible, after the feasibility of the railway or on the Danube. We have also advocated strengthening the border regions – I can't do that in all the details now – as well as a sustainable environmental policy; I do not do so in detail. The question of security has only been added to the conclusion and has perhaps become too strong a focus in the overall context. But one thing has to be said quite clearly: First of all, what it is all about is the recognition that the candidate countries are already making a very important contribution to our security today, because they have to take on some of the burdens that we otherwise have to bear, even now, in the run-up to enlargement. Consider asylum law and the fact that most of our neighbouring countries are so-called safe third countries in most cases. Let me say one last thing: The last point that has been included in our paper is agriculture. He has been included at the request of the ÖVP, and on this point we have extraordinarily... – The Greens claim that it has been added to their wishes. [[Abg. Dr. Lichtenberger: We were!]] You probably won't be particularly satisfied with the result, we're not either. [[Abg. Dr. Lichtenberger: I fear that too!]] Ladies and gentlemen! High house! That is what it is all about, that this motion for a resolution, the three parties, the two government parties and we, are ready to carry together and are able to create foundations that allow for a genuinely serious preparation of enlargement and that also allow the Austrians to say: Don't be afraid of enlargement! The necessary steps are being taken! Enlargement is a project to which we say yes!

ParlaMint-AT_2001-11-21-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00083_d7e18662	Mr President. Mr State Secretary! Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! The Chancellor of the Federal Republic of Germany has today accused a Member of having gone into a serious issue of "light footing". I am referring here to a question in which the Federal Chancellor himself, in order to stick to his words, has entered very lightly, namely, on 26 March. The question of Austrian neutrality was raised in October. However, I do not want my speaking time to compare Mr Bundeskanzler, to which a lot of things would also come to mind – especially amusing – with regard to neutrality itself, but it is important for me to highlight a detailed aspect of neutrality here in this House in connection with Nice. In the Committee's deliberations on the Nice Treaty, the German Chancellor, as has already been said, claimed that the Austrian Constitution had been changed after accession to the European Union and following the Treaty of Amsterdam in such a way that the Austrian neutrality in the field of European security and defence policy would no longer apply. In legal terms, the Federal Chancellor claims that Article 23f B-VG would have repealed the Federal Constitutional Act on Austrian Neutrality for this area. The Socialist Group does not share this view. This view is also, as I have said, represented by the Chancellor of the Federal Republic of Germany only since the Social Democrats – as guardians of neutrality – are no longer represented in the Federal Government. For us, the legal situation that we ourselves have brought is as follows: Austria has joined the European Union as a neutral state. As has always been stressed by Foreign Minister Mock, a reservation of neutrality was not necessary because the Austrian Neutrality Act continued to apply and was also accepted by every EU State. Only a constitutional basis has been established for the limitation of economic relations for which competence has been transferred to the EU. The Treaty of Amsterdam extended the common European security and defence policy, in particular the so-called Petersberg tasks, including armed efforts to create peace. These peacekeeping operations are now relevant in so far as they could be a international war, but they do not have to be. They are not a war within the meaning of international law if they are based on a mandate of the UN or the OSCE. In this case, a neutral state like Austria can also be involved. Article 23f of the B-VG on the participation of Austria in the common European security and defence policy was subsequently amended accordingly by mentioning the Petersberg tasks. This does not mean, however, that insofar as the Neutrality Act was restricted, it is rather true that Austria can only participate in Petersberg tasks if this is compatible with the Neutrality Act. This was not a retroactive thing for us now, since we Social Democrats are no longer in the government, but I already have it on 18 November. In my speech on the ratification of the Treaty of Amsterdam in June 1998, I also said in this direction here in the National Council. The claim that the Neutrality Act has now been detogulated is already impermissible because a degration ratio requires that the two standards have the same scope. But this is not the case! Instead, the Neutrality Act restricts Article 23f B-VG by restricting Austria's participation in Petersberg tasks only if this is compatible with neutrality. This position of neutral EU Member States is also explicitly taken into account by the Treaty of Amsterdam. Article 17 of the EU Treaty, which introduced these Petersberg tasks, explicitly takes into account the specific nature of the security and defence policy of certain Member States. This turn is known as the "Irish clause", precisely because it will take account of the neutral status of EU Member States. Article 23(1) of the EU Treaty also provided for a special instrument enabling neutral states to maintain their status, i.e. the so-called "constructive censure". And the continuation of the Neutrality Act obliges the Austrian Federal Government to make use of this "constructive abstinence" if necessary. Federal Chancellor Schüssel and Klubobmann Khol would like to refer to the Committee's report or to the reference to Article 51 of the Charter of the United Nations in this Committee report. – There is no argument from this! Article 51 merely regulates the right to self-defense of each State. Of course, Austria is not neutral even if it itself is attacked and has to defend itself. What is behind this legal discussion, ladies and gentlemen, I think is quite different: ÖVP and FPÖ have politically adopted themselves of neutrality, for them the Neutrality Act is only more an obstacle. Therefore, every argument is right – including this – to eliminate the neutrality law. The Federal Chancellor is no comparison to embarrassing to lower it. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] Ladies and gentlemen! We Socialists face this! For us, the Neutrality Act is not only legally binding, but also the basis of Austrian policy within and outside the European Union.

ParlaMint-AT_2001-11-21-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00083_d7e19517	We're now on the 9th. Agenda item. We are going into the debate. First of all, Mrs Mag. Muttons. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2001-11-21-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00083_d7e19545	Mr President. Mrs. Minister! High house! We've already heard: This draft government, the Second Protocol to the 1954 Hague Convention, deals with the protection of cultural property. This Second Protocol expresses the need to improve the protection of cultural property in the event of armed conflicts and to create a stronger, better protection system for particularly marked cultural goods. But also the need to safeguard the cultural heritage by creating directories, planning emergency measures and preparing the relocation of movable cultural goods has been taken into account. The basic idea behind this Hague Convention and this Protocol is that outstanding testimonies of human cultural creation belong not only to the States in whose territory they are located, but to the heritage of all humanity and constitute a cultural legacy. Every country signing this Convention commits itself to respect and preserve the cultural heritage for future generations. However, cultural goods are not only witnesses of cultural creation, but also expressions of the cultural identity of a people and therefore play a special role in war, ethnic or religious conflicts, for this identity is extremely vulnerable, and a weakening of this identity is intended to harm the opponent by the deliberate destruction of cultural goods. Think of the war in the Balkans. There, numerous mosques or orthodox churches became victims of an inconceivable destruction. These experiences have led, among other things, to the fact that in the Additional Protocol to the Hague Convention, the intentional destruction of cultural goods in the case of war in the future is a criminal offence and criminal prosecution. Cultural goods as cultural heritage are and remain a very fragile and fragile asset. Unfortunately, absolute security for this is excluded, but awareness-building measures aimed at a solidarity of the States are of enormous importance, even though we must be aware that the effect of international agreements in principle is based only on the commitment of the States which accede to these agreements. There are, for example, the Hague Convention, but there is also the 1972 UNESCO Convention for the Protection of the World's Cultural and Natural Heritage. As you know, it has also become possible that the 1,500-year-old Buddhist statues were destroyed in the Bamian Valley and the international community of this insanity state had to stand by. The UNESCO, which accepts the preservation of cultural goods, was also powerless. What UNESCO does, however, is to put experience, knowledge and moral weight into play. It can move States to cooperate, including within the framework of such international conventions. The signatory states can then also expect the support and expertise of UNESCO, for example in securing their own cultural goods or in helping them in the organisational field. The so-called "red list" of the World Heritage Site shows how difficult the situation in this area is, which is presented annually by the UNESCO Intergovernmental Secretariat for particularly endangered cultural sites. With the entry into this "Red List", UNESCO aims to raise the attention of the politically responsible and public interest, i.e. awareness-raising. Ladies and gentlemen! Cultural policy is a global task. In culture there is a lot of potential for dealing with crisis situations. Not for nothing have the United Nations proclaimed the year 2001 as the year of dialogue between cultures. A key prerequisite for preventing conflicts is deep inner cultural understanding and respect for foreign cultures, both in their own country and in the interstate. Therefore, this Second Protocol is an important step in the protection of cultural goods and a sign of the appreciation of culture. - Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2001-11-21-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00083_d7e19617	Mr President. Thank you, Mrs. Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! Samuel Huntington's famous book "The Clash of Civilizations" has just finished 11th. This year again, a terrifying up-to-date event. He himself says in the interview in the "time": "The attack" on the World Trade Center was first and foremost "an attack of common barbarians on the civilized... world. ... It is important that this crime... does not trigger the struggle of cultures." ==References== The Hague Convention on the Protection of Cultural Property in Armed Conflicts was signed in May 1954. This Convention entered into force for Austria in 1964. The former Yugoslavia also joined in 1956. Nevertheless, the historic port of Dubrovnik was destroyed in 1991. ==References== By the way, the International Court of Justice raised the attacks within the meaning of the Hague Convention as a charge. The Buddha statues have already been mentioned. Here again, the current events surrounding this destruction of the 1,500-year-old Buddhist statues by the Taliban regime are another dilemma of cultural policy struggles. But like no other event, the Second World War has destroyed the European cultural landscape in this century. Art treasures were torn from the collections and brought to new places – not to talk about losses. A particularly striking example is the "Schatz des Priamos", which was lowered and transported to the Soviet Union by the so-called Trophies Commissions in occupied Germany. This raises the question: Do cultural goods also play a special role in wars or in ethnic-religious conflicts? - Yes! This phenomenon, ladies and gentlemen, is as old as culture itself. You want to attack the opponent. One wants to attack him where he is most vulnerable – in his cultural identity. ==References== In May 1999, Austria signed the Second Protocol on the occasion of the 100th anniversary of the Hague Peace Conference. This now establishes the following: an enhanced protection for particularly protected cultural property – the condition is the creation of a list – a precision of the extremely vaguely kept exception clause of a compelling military necessity and, finally, a unique change, because serious violations of the Convention such as attack, destruction, but also appropriation, theft, looting, misrepresentation or malicious damage can now be individually prosecuted. The Contracting Parties undertake, on the basis of the crime catalogue of the International Criminal Court's status, to suspect certain offences or to extradite the perpetrators. So far, 30 states have signed. Ladies and gentlemen! Cultural policy is a central challenge of our time. Only with the help of allies will it be possible for us to avoid the struggle of cultures and to engage in a dialogue of cultures.

Now a little swivel on another subject that we have already discussed today: Of course, we could also say that Lipizzans and Mozart balls are identical to a society, but I would already have problems classifying them among the cultural goods. With neutrality I would probably have a problem, even if it is quite identical, but it does not belong to the cultural heritage within the meaning of this Hague Convention. Back to the convention itself. The positive thing about this convention is that the exception clauses have now been clarified. When cultural goods are "used" in military terms – under quotation marks – when churches are used when mosques are practically, so that there is no attack there, there can be exception clauses. What has also been clarified is that non-governmental organisations can make proposals for the list of cultural goods that can be reported. I think this is a great progress, because especially in countries in which civil society is not yet so marked, or in those in which it is very well, it makes sense that civil society and not only the state can propose cultural goods to be included in this list. [[Congratulation with the greens.]] The increased protection of particularly protected cultural goods has also been extended. This includes, of course, moving cultural goods. What is a useful point in this new law is the fact that international organisations such as the Unesco are involved, which can also contribute to the absence of myth formations. As an example, I mention all that has evolved from myths around the treasures or even non-treasures in the Toplitzsee or around the amber room that had disappeared during World War I and from which parts now appear again. With all these movable cultural goods, it makes sense for them to be entered in a list in a timely manner, that is, in times of peace, but completely decreed when an armed conflict begins, if it is clear what it is about, and if – and this is the other important point in this law – it is also clear that the perpetrators, the perpetrators who are extradited and must be prosecuted, that thus those persons, whether they are soldiers, whether they are looters, or others, who are responsible for the destruction, should be forced to be extradited. Of course, despite all the protection of cultural goods, it should be noted that people themselves should be at the centre of the efforts of protection, because without the protection of people everything else becomes marginal. I also refer to the destruction of the Buddha statues in Afghanistan. There's been a cry in the world. There has not been a cry for what was done by the Taliban regime in front of it, especially in women. So without the protection of human beings, the protection of the cultural property will probably also be marginal, yet I note that an important point in this law is that there should be more awareness. In this regard, I have thought about what it was like in my school years, whether I've ever heard something about what it looks like with cultural goods or what to protect. It's been a long time since I was in school, but demands have shown that this is not really common even in recent times. That is why I believe that there should be more action in this area also in the field of curriculum, in the field of schools, to inform young people about how cultural goods should be handled, that they are at all available and that they are safe. [[Congratulation with the greens.]] In this sense, we too will agree to this request.

ParlaMint-AT_2001-11-21-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00083_d7e20671	Mr President. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! ==References== In January 1999, the Mineral Crude Law, which replaced the Berggesetz 1975, largely changed the legal situation for the Austrian mining industry. The first report of the Federal Minister for Economic Affairs and Labour has shown that some of the provisions of the Mineral Raw Materials Act are complicated and complete and also require a high administrative burden. The legislative amendment therefore provides for simplification and completion of the provisions on the extraction of mineral raw materials. The opposition has been particularly stressed that a change has taken place in the tones or in the bleaches of the government. So far, we have had a division in the tones: In Blähtonen, the Bergbehörde or the Ministry was responsible, and in the other tones it was the county captain. Since there are often different tones in a mining area, this has led to a double government work. Extensive opinions were needed. In order to simplify this, we have provided for a uniform authorisation for the tones, which will be valid in the future. I also welcome § 151a for the following reason. If land transfers are forced to be ordered, these land must be returned to the previous owner, so that no hortung is possible for a longer period of time, if there has not begun or is standing at the door within 15 years. Mr Mag. Kurt Gaßner! On the accusations you have just made here, I would like to mention once again that the absolute prohibition range of 100 metres is not affected. So the gravel pit does not come closer to the citizen; that is not the case. It is therefore not possible to take advantage of the reduction of houses and so on. A reduction in the 300-metre range is also possible at present, when it comes to motorways, highways or railway tracks, even if a deterioration in the situation of the Immission occurs with the neighbours. In the future, reduction should only be possible if special local or landscape conditions, construction facilities or decommissioning measures allow for shorter distances. They have also mentioned quarries, where this is not at all possible, because such a reduction is not permitted in the case of rock mining. Either the novelle was not read exactly, or one has only called on press releases. In fact, the new regulations will take far better account of environmental and neighbourhood protection than the current regulations. With regard to the loss of the party position of the neighboring community, I must expressly stress here that those in § 81 para. 2 MinroG regulated party position of neighbors remains intact.

The cost savings will be around 2.1 billion schilling, certainly a considerable sum, but of course it is not the 30 percent that have been raised, because the starting point is 1999 and we now have quite different basic conditions and other member numbers. This is only for the sake of accuracy! By the way: That there is no longer the registration fee, which we abolished some time ago, is also very useful, because in the past it was a real contradiction that we talked about promoting young entrepreneurs and then, when someone dared to jump into self-employment, as a greeting gift, he almost missed the "integration fee". But now on the second point of view, to my comments as Vice-President of the Lower Austria Chamber of Commerce, as regards the rules of the game from this point of view. In the final effect, we make the decision of the chamber day of 30. ==References====External links== This decision has, of course, preceded many deliberations within the Chamber, because it has been taken very seriously, ladies and gentlemen. As a special aspect, the idea of partnership federalism, on which we in particular place the greatest importance in the Chambers of State, on the other hand, is the acceleration of the election, and that is not to be underestimated, the maintenance of autonomy. It is very welcome that the term of election is reduced from three months to one month and that the composition of the Economic Parliament is already fixed on the eve of the election. As a minority group, however, it is even more important to us that the new right to vote is essential – and this must be admitted openly and honestly – more minority-friendly than the old one. The new 5 percent minority regime would give us some more mandates in Lower Austria at the present state of the votes. Ladies and gentlemen! A very important aspect for us, however, is the Rules of Procedure, which, although not directly related to the Economic Chamber Act, is an important part of the daily working conditions. Here, too, we have found great understanding in the majority group, and I would like to thank Mr Karlheinz Kopf and Dr. Kreimer for the constructive discussions on this occasion. [[Demonstrative applause with the ÖVP.]] Ladies and gentlemen! I can assure you in return that we will honour this fair attitude through constructive chamberwork. By the way, I would also like to say on this occasion that my group, the Economic Union, has not only been loyal to the Chamber of Commerce for decades, but has also worked with great commitment in all the bodies and groups of the Federal and Country Chambers. As far as the basics are concerned, it is in our fullest sense that membership of the Union is not a matter of complaint, because only in this way the guarantee is given for a strong representation of interests. In this sense, my friends and I want to make a clear commitment to the Austrian social partners. On this occasion, I personally would also like to thank my own group very much for giving its approval to the law.

ParlaMint-AT_2001-11-21-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00083_d7e25345	Mr President. Federal Minister! My predecessor, Mr Maier, has not spoken about this point of order at the end of his remarks, but has rightly stated that the question of the European arrest warrant will also have to be discussed in a parliamentary way. It is an intervention in existing constitutional law, and it is certainly not a small matter when it is discussed at European level that the Republic of Austria should undertake to grant extradition requests from other Member States regarding Austrian citizens, including crimes that are punishable abroad but not in Austria. This would be a fundamental change in our legal principles, and it is only legitimate for this to be presented to the House and negotiated here. Now the actual law, the e-commerce law. – In the beginning, there will be only two types of people: "the connected" and "the unconnected". This is the bitter fact that emerges from the booming Internet. On the one hand, those connected to this medium are able to deal with and use this medium – on the other hand, those who are "unconnected" and do not have these possibilities. This is the bitter side of the medal that it will not be accessible to all because not everyone will deal with it. On the other hand, the Internet is a fascinating medium for me, because it links unimagined information possibilities. Just a few years ago, it would have been totally unthinkable to find information on the Internet with the mere input of anything – as it is said – that could only be dreamed of in the past. It is amazing and remarkable how the Internet consolidates itself in everyday life, not only by e-mail traffic, but also quite simply by the possibilities you have as a lawyer, for example, through the use of the legal information system, which is still available free of charge and free of charge, or by the use of databases – whether it is the company book or the base book; I also address Eurolex. At the touch of a button you can have in all languages all the translations of all directives or directives at European level, any case in point, including any case of the European Court of Justice in Luxembourg, the Court of First Instance and the European Court of Human Rights. This is really something incredibly fascinating, and it improves the quality of legal advice also in other areas. However, it is also necessary to create a legal framework for the use of the Internet and e-commerce. This law implements the existing directive. In my opinion, the implementation of this directive proves once again that the original idea of a right-free area of the Internet is completely absurd and unfair, for: The law is Internet-neutral, which means that all legislation can also be applied to the Internet and the content that is being disseminated there. There are some special provisions that are now introduced with this government template, such as the liability in the setting of links, which of course must be very modest, because each link means an extension of information possibilities, any site can be changed at random, and one cannot assume to the owner of a homepage that it monitors how all links develop. But then, if an illegality is found, there must be the possibility of entering and drawing attention to the provider: This link must be deleted immediately because it intervenes in personality rights because it violates or violates copyright. This also provides for this government template, in addition to the establishment principle in the so-called coordinated area, in the area directly covered by the Directive and being implemented vertically in each Member State. I think it is very necessary to create some legal certainty for those who offer services on the Internet so that they can rely on it: If he fulfils the right of his own state – where he places the content on the Internet – he simultaneously fulfils all obligations. It is very important that this country of origin principle is not bad, but only limited in the coordinated area. In other areas, the sovereignty of the Member States – and this is also good – remains a coordinated area, as is the case with the TV directive on cross-border television, in which exclusively the right of the domestic programme provider applies, but where serious infringements take place, for example, in the content – risk of youth and so on – the law of the individual state is also to be observed.
